=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a grid of N^2 squares with N rows and N columns.
Let (i,j) denote the square at the i-th row from the top (1\leq i\leq N) and j-th column from the left (1\leq j\leq N).
Each square is either empty or has a piece placed on it.
There are M pieces placed on the grid, and the k-th (1\leq k\leq M) piece is placed on square (a_k,b_k).
You want to place your piece on an empty square in such a way that it cannot be captured by any of the existing pieces.
A piece placed on square (i,j) can capture pieces that satisfy any of the following conditions:

- Placed in row i
- Placed in column j
- Placed on any square (a,b)\ (1\leq a\leq N,1\leq b\leq N) where i+j=a+b
- Placed on any square (a,b)\ (1\leq a\leq N,1\leq b\leq N) where i-j=a-b

For example, a piece placed on square (4,4) can capture pieces placed on the squares shown in blue in the following figure:

How many squares can you place your piece on?

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
a_1 b_1
a_2 b_2
\vdots
a_M b_M

Output

Print the number of empty squares where you can place your piece without it being captured by any existing pieces.

Constraints


- 1\leq N\leq10^9
- 1\leq M\leq10^3
- 1\leq a_k\leq N,1\leq b_k\leq N\ (1\leq k\leq M)
- (a_k,b_k)\neq(a_l,b_l)\ (1\leq k\lt l\leq M)
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

8 6
1 4
2 1
3 8
4 5
5 2
8 3

Sample Output 1

2

The existing pieces can capture pieces placed on the squares shown in blue in the following figure:

Therefore, you can place your piece on only two squares: squares (6,6) and (7,7).

Sample Input 2

1000000000 1
1 1

Sample Output 2

999999997000000002

Out of 10^{18} squares, the squares that cannot be used are: squares in row 1, squares in column 1, and squares (1,1), (2,2), \ldots, (10^9,10^9), totaling 3\times10^9-2 squares.
Note that the answer may be 2^{32} or greater.

Sample Input 3

20 10
1 4
7 11
7 15
8 10
11 6
12 5
13 1
15 2
20 10
20 15

Sample Output 3

77

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Now union formula with overlap count: For each main diagonal count_main_A is number of cells on that diagonal in A (i notin R, j notin C). For each anti diagonal similarly. We can compute.

Now need compute B_count = sum count_main_A(d) + sum count_anti_A(s) - sum_{d,s} intersection_A(d,s).

But wait: Are main diagonals D1 and anti diagonals D2 sets from pieces. What if a cell is on a forbidden main diagonal and a forbidden anti diagonal; counted in both sums, subtract once. Good. Potential issue: The same cell might be intersection of a main diagonal d and an anti diagonal s where d is in D1 and s in D2. But what if that cell is also on another main diagonal? no. Good.

Let's write functions:

def count_main(d):
    L = max(1, 1 + d)
    U = min(N, N + d)
    if L > U:
        return 0
    length = U - L + 1
    row_rem = 0
    for r in R:
        if L <= r <= U:
            row_rem += 1
    col_rem = 0
    for c in C:
        i = c + d
        if L <= i <= U:
            col_rem += 1
    overlap = 0
    for r in R:
        if L <= r <= U and (r - d) in C:
            overlap += 1
    return length - row_rem - col_rem + overlap

But note row_rem and overlap both iterate R; can combine to reduce:
row_rem=0; overlap=0
for r in R:
  if L <= r <= U:
    row_rem += 1
    if (r - d) in C: overlap +=1
Then col_rem iterate C. This is O(|R|+|C|). Good.

Similarly anti:
def count_anti(s):
    L = max(1, s - N)
    U = min(N, s - 1)
    if L > U: return 0
    length = U - L + 1
    row_rem=0; overlap=0
    for r in R:
       if L <= r <= U:
          row_rem +=1
          if (s - r) in C: overlap +=1
    col_rem=0
    for c in C:
       i = s - c
       if L <= i <= U:
          col_rem +=1
    return length - row_rem - col_rem + overlap

Check anti overlap condition: cell (r, s-r) with row r in R and column s-r in C. Need r in [L,U] ensures column within [1,N]. If s-r in C, then column forbidden. Good. But what if r in R and s-r in C but r not in [L,U]? If r in [1,N] and c=s-r in [1,N], then r is in [L,U] automatically as argued. But check with L=max(1,s-N), U=min(N,s-1). If r+c=s, c>=1 => r<=s-1; c<=N => r>=s-N; r in [1,N]. So yes. But checking L<=r<=U is safe. If r in R but not in [L,U], then cell not on diagonal within grid; but if c=s-r in C and c in [1,N], then r would be in [L,U]. So no issue.

Similarly main overlap: if r in R and r-d in C, then r in [L,U] automatically. But check safe.

Now intersection_A(d,s):
if (s + d) % 2 != 0: continue
i = (s + d) // 2
j = (s - d) // 2
if 1 <= i <= N and 1 <= j <= N:
    if i not in R and j not in C:
        B -= 1? Actually B_count = sum_main + sum_anti - intersections. We can compute total = main_sum + anti_sum; then subtract intersection_count. So in loop if valid and i notin R and j notin C: intersection_count +=1. Then B = main_sum + anti_sum - intersection_count.

Need ensure i,j integers. Since s+d parity. If d,s have same parity? i,j integer iff s+d even and s-d even, equivalent s and d same parity. If not, no intersection cell with integer coordinates. Also if i/j outside grid, no cell. If i/j inside but row/col forbidden, not in A, no subtract.

Now sample 3 maybe trust.

But let's think of edge cases and potential pitfalls.

1. N up to 1e9, M up to 1000. Use Python int.

2. D1 set may contain negative values. L/U formulas handle.

3. D2 set values from 2 to 2N. L/U formulas handle.

4. If N=1, D2={2}; anti L=max(1,1)=1,U=min(1,1)=1 length1. Good.

5. If M pieces share row/col/diagonal, R,C,D sets unique. But D1/D2 sets should include all diagonals with at least one piece. If multiple pieces on same diagonal, set.

6. If a piece is on same row and column? no duplicates.

7. Count of empty squares can be huge up to 1e18, fits Python.

Now, is the union formula for B_count complete? We count cells in A that are on at least one forbidden diagonal. Let U = union of main diagonal sets M_d for d∈D1 and anti sets A_s for s∈D2, all restricted to A. Since M_d disjoint across d, A_s disjoint across s. The union size = sum |M_d| + sum |A_s| - sum_{d,s} |M_d ∩ A_s|. Is there any double counting between M_d and A_s beyond pairwise? A point belongs to exactly one M_d and one A_s (if any). The inclusion-exclusion for union of two families where each family is disjoint: The formula is standard. More formally, for each cell, let x=1 if covered by some main, y=1 if covered by some anti. It contributes to main sum x, anti sum y, intersection sum x*y. Union indicator x∨y = x + y - x*y. Good. No triple because x,y binary. So correct.

Need ensure count_main_A(d) counts all cells on diagonal d in A, not just those not attacked by other diagonals. Yes.

Now let's consider alternative direct count maybe but formula good.

N=3, pieces (1,1) and (2,2). R={1,2}, C={1,2}, D1={0}, D2={2,4}. |A|=(1)*(1)=1 cell (3,3). Is (3,3) attacked? main d=0 yes, so safe 0. B: main d=0 length3, row_rem=2, col_rem=2, overlap=2 (r=1,2 in C) => count=1. anti s=2 length1 =>0; s=4 length2? cells (1,3),(2,2),(3,1)? Wait i+j=4: (1,3),(2,2),(3,1) length3? Let's compute L=max(1,1)=1,U=min(3,3)=3 length3. row_rem rows 1,2 =>2. col_rem c with 4-c in [1,3]: c=1,2,3? c=1 i=3 in [1,3], c=2 i=2, c=3 i=1; C={1,2} =>2. overlap r in R [1,3] with 4-r in C: r=1->3 no,2->2 yes =>1. count=3-2-2+1=0. Wait anti s=4 cells: (1,3) row1, (2,2) row2/col2, (3,1) col1, all forbidden. count0. Intersections d=0,s=2 cell (1,1) not A; d=0,s=4 cell (2,2) not A. B=1. safe=0. Good.

Now, let's think about row/col overlap count in count_main_A. We compute overlap as number of r in R with L<=r<=U and (r-d) in C. But what if r-d in C but r-d outside [1,N]? If r in [L,U], then r-d in [L-d,U-d] = [max(1-d,1), min(N-d,N)] subset [1,N]. So okay. If we didn't check L<=r<=U, could count invalid; we do.

Similarly anti overlap: r in [L,U] and s-r in C. If r in [L,U], s-r in [s-U,s-L] = [max(1,s-N)? let's derive: L=max(1,s-N), U=min(N,s-1). s-U >= s - min(N,s-1) = max(s-N,1). s-L <= s - max(1,s-N) = min(s-1,N). So column within [1,N]. Good.

Now, col_rem for main: for c in C, i=c+d. Need L<=i<=U. But if c+d maybe not int? c,d ints. Good. If i in [L,U], then cell (i,c) on diagonal. If c in C. Good.

Now, count_main_A formula can be negative? Shouldn't. Let's test edge where row_rem and col_rem overlap count computed by iterating R. Is overlap count equal to number of cells counted in both row_rem and col_rem? row_rem counts rows r∈R∩[L,U]. col_rem counts columns c∈C such that c+d∈[L,U]. A cell is in both if its row r∈R∩[L,U] and its column c=r-d∈C. Since if c∈C and c+d=r∈[L,U], then col_rem includes c. So overlap count = |{r∈R∩[L,U] : r-d∈C}|. Yes.

Now, for anti, overlap = |{r∈R∩[L,U] : s-r∈C}|. Good.

Now, could row_rem and col_rem overlap at a cell whose row/col are both forbidden but the cell is also on the diagonal; we add back. Good.

Now, let's consider if D1/D2 sets contain diagonal values that are not actually occupied? We build from pieces, so yes.

Now, maybe there is a simpler formula using inclusion-exclusion over all lines? But our approach is solid.

Let's think about performance and memory.

Input reading:
import sys
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
N=data[0]; M=data[1]
R=set(); C=set(); D1=set(); D2=set()
idx=2
for _ in range(M):
    a=data[idx]; b=data[idx+1]; idx+=2
    R.add(a); C.add(b); D1.add(a-b); D2.add(a+b)

Then compute.

Potential issue: D1 set can include values from pieces; if a piece's main diagonal is same as another, set. D2 set.

Now, count_main_sum:
main_sum=0
for d in D1:
    L = 1 + d
    if L < 1: L = 1
    U = N + d
    if U > N: U = N
    if L > U: continue
    length = U - L + 1
    row_rem=0; overlap=0
    for r in R:
        if L <= r <= U:
            row_rem += 1
            if (r - d) in C:
                overlap += 1
    col_rem=0
    for c in C:
        i = c + d
        if L <= i <= U:
            col_rem += 1
    main_sum += length - row_rem - col_rem + overlap

Intersection_count:
inter=0
for d in D1:
    for s in D2:
        if ((s + d) & 1): continue
        i = (s + d) // 2
        j = (s - d) // 2
        if 1 <= i <= N and 1 <= j <= N:
            if i not in R and j not in C:
                inter += 1

Let's write final code:

import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    R = set()
    C = set()
    D1 = set()
    D2 = set()
    idx = 2
    for _ in range(M):
        a = data[idx]; b = data[idx+1]; idx += 2
        R.add(a)
        C.add(b)
        D1.add(a - b)
        D2.add(a + b)

    main_sum = 0
    for d in D1:
        L = 1 + d
        if L < 1:
            L = 1
        U = N + d
        if U > N:
            U = N
        if L > U:
            continue
        length = U - L + 1
        row_rem = 0
        overlap = 0
        for r in R:
            if L <= r <= U:
                row_rem += 1
                if (r - d) in C:
                    overlap += 1
        col_rem = 0
        for c in C:
            i = c + d
            if L <= i <= U:
                col_rem += 1
        main_sum += length - row_rem - col_rem + overlap

    anti_sum = 0
    for s in D2:
        L = s - N
        if L < 1:
            L = 1
        U = s - 1
        if U > N:
            U = N
        if L > U:
            continue
        length = U - L + 1
        row_rem = 0
        overlap = 0
        for r in R:
            if L <= r <= U:
                row_rem += 1
                if (s - r) in C:
                    overlap += 1
        col_rem = 0
        for c in C:
            i = s - c
            if L <= i <= U:
                col_rem += 1
        anti_sum += length - row_rem - col_rem + overlap

    inter = 0
    for d in D1:
        for s in D2:
            if (s + d) & 1:
                continue
            i = (s + d) // 2
            j = (s - d) // 2
            if 1 <= i <= N and 1 <= j <= N:
                if i not in R and j not in C:
                    inter += 1

    total_allowed_rows_cols = (N - len(R)) * (N - len(C))
    forbidden_by_diag = main_sum + anti_sum - inter
    ans = total_allowed_rows_cols - forbidden_by_diag
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

=== ANSWER ===
: "import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return

    N, M = data[0], data[1]

    rows = set()
    cols = set()
    diag1 = set()  # i - j
    diag2 = set()  # i + j

    idx = 2
    for _ in range(M):
        a = data[idx]
        b = data[idx + 1]
        idx += 2
        rows.add(a)
        cols.add(b)
        diag1.add(a - b)
        diag2.add(a + b)

    # Count cells on forbidden main diagonals that are not in forbidden rows/columns.
    main_sum = 0
    for d in diag1:
        L = max(1, 1 + d)
        U = min(N, N + d)
        if L > U:
            continue

        length = U - L + 1

        row_rem = 0
        overlap = 0
        for r in rows:
            if L <= r <= U:
                row_rem += 1
                if (r - d) in cols:
                    overlap += 1

        col_rem = 0
        for c in cols:
            i = c + d
            if L <= i <= U:
                col_rem += 1

        main_sum += length - row_rem - col_rem + overlap

    # Count cells on forbidden anti diagonals that are not in forbidden rows/columns.
    anti_sum = 0
    for s in diag2:
        L = max(1, s - N)
        U = min(N, s - 1)
        if L > U:
            continue

        length = U - L + 1

        row_rem = 0
        overlap = 0
        for r in rows:
            if L <= r <= U:
                row_rem += 1
                if (s - r) in cols:
                    overlap += 1

        col_rem = 0
        for c in cols:
            i = s - c
            if L <= i <= U:
                col_rem += 1

        anti_sum += length - row_rem - col_rem + overlap

    # Subtract cells counted on both a main and an anti diagonal.
    inter = 0
    for d in diag1:
        for s in diag2:
            if (s + d) & 1:
                continue
            i = (s + d) // 2
            j = (s - d) // 2
            if 1 <= i <= N and 1 <= j <= N:
                if i not in rows and j not in cols:
                    inter += 1

    allowed_by_row_col = (N - len(rows)) * (N - len(cols))
    forbidden_by_diag = main_sum + anti_sum - inter

    ans = allowed_by_row_col - forbidden_by_diag
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
" }
